#5069. AI agent disputes head to ‘Internet Court’ — and a jury of AI agents

Who better to judge a dispute between AI agents than a jury of different AI models? That’s the idea behind GenLayer, which is already issuing thousands of judgements each day.

The world is headed towards a future in which AI agents will trade stocks and crypto, shop for your family, and much more. But what happens when a bot misinterprets an instruction to buy $10K of “blue chip stocks” and instead buys $10K of Bluebird-brand frozen chips?

Low-level agent disputes like this aren’t suited to the expensive and time-consuming existing legal system. Enter Internet Court, a new protocol that enables agents and humans to enter into contracts, with funds kept in escrow until the terms are judged to have been met.

The dispute resolution mechanism baked into the process employs GenLayer, an open, delegated proof-of-stake network of validators hooked up to AI models.

Operating in beta for the past year, it currently processes around 12,000 decisions per day. It authenticates user-uploaded photos for Collective Memory and resolves arguments on argue.fun. Mainnet is expected to launch next month.

The concept is a modern spin on the famous jury theorem proposed by philosopher Nicolas de Condorcet who argued the probability of reaching the correct answers increases as the number of independent evaluators grows.For Internet Court that means setting up a jury of AI agents from various models in the Claude, Gemini, ChatGPT and DeepSeek families, along with a range of others.

“It’s not making the decision by using a single AI that would have some biases,” explains co-founder and CEO of GenLayer Albert Castellana. “It’s actually using a lot of different AIs, up to 1,500 different AIs. And when that happens, you have essentially better responses.”

“One is left-leaning, the other one is right-leaning. That’s how you get a better answer because one cancels the other.”

Disputes are resolved by sending the details to a randomly selected jury of five GenLayer validators, all running a different AI model. An initial decision is reached within minutes and is based on a simple majority vote, but the decision can be appealed to a wider jury of 11 agents, and then 23, and more and more until you hit the system’s limit.Castellana says finality typically occurs within 30 minutes. “If you wait until maximum finality, it is escalating rounds of appeal. You start with five, and it escalates to fifteen hundred, which could be three hours.”

The Court has 35 members in its open consortium so far, including well-known crypto firms such as ZKsync, NEAR, Consensys and UMA — which resolves disputes for Polymarket.

GenLayer has been in development for almost four years, and began life as an attempt to come up with a better way to evaluate what information is worthy of inclusion in an online knowledge base like Wikipedia.

In theory, anyone is supposed to be able to correct mistakes in the online encyclopedia but in practice, controversial topics from crypto to the war in Gaza get locked, with a small group of powerful editors getting the final say on contested information.

“The original idea behind GenLayer came with […] how could we have a decentralized Wikipedia?” he explains.

Related: Your fridge could one day stake Ether, launch memecoins — CFTC chair

GenLayer enables a new form of smart contract dubbed an “intelligent contract” that allows for subjective judgment. For example, you could contract a freelance writer and allow the system to automatically judge if their content is any good before paying them.

“We have a platform doing exactly this — paying influencers to write content online and then getting the money out,” he explains.

“Intelligent contracts can do code. They can do natural language. They can go to the internet. They can understand an image. They can have judgment,” he says.

The extent to which you can rely on an AI’s judgment in 2026 is limited by the capabilities of the technology. A standup comedian probably wouldn’t hire a joke writer via an intelligent contract.

Castellana explains the tech is also not good enough — yet — to judge whether a picture is a deepfake, or to resolve a boundary dispute between neighbors based on photos. It will certainly give you an answer, but you shouldn’t rely on it just yet. But there are plenty of occasions where AI judgments are already good enough. GenLayer automatically assesses photos uploaded by users to reality mapping platform Collective Memory for quality and accuracy and decides on a payment.

The other big user is Argue.fun, which enables users to bet on the resolution of arguments that are settled by lengthy debates between agents. The top four users have earned more than $2 million each. Most of the questions to date relate to serious policy issues around GenLayer governance, DAOs and AI safety.

Within the next few years, Castellana sees this process paving the way for genuinely autonomous DAOs that can automatically assess proposals according to the DAO’s constitution and wider aims.

GenLayer launches mainnet next month. Source: GenLayer

A more near-term use case is to resolve prediction market disputes more quickly. In mid-2025, Polymarket was sent into a spin over an ambiguously worded $240 million market on whether Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky would wear a suit before July. When he turned up at the June NATO summit wearing an outfit that was vaguely suit-like, the market initially resolved to “Yes.” But after nine days of appeals and back and forth among UMA token holder voters, the decision flipped to “No.”Castellana says GenLayer could shorten that period considerably.”It’s going to be resolved in a matter of minutes, and you will have hundreds, or thousands of AIs that make that vote very, very quickly. So at the end of the day, what you want is a neutral adjudication layer. You want something that is fast, accessible, and cheap.”

Whether humans will accept the decisions is another question entirely. But Internet Court and GenLayer aren’t designed to provide the final judgment, and decisions can still be appealed to humans.”After that, if you are still disagreeing and still think that Zelensky is not wearing a suit, what you can do is basically take it to Kleros, UMA, or even to a human court.”

Outsourcing dispute resolution to AI models comes with some big risks. The first is that the technology is just too immature to produce reliable judgments.

Another problem is that AI models reproduce many of the biases they find in the training data — and to date, the tech firms’ attempts to redress those biases have sometimes just flipped the bias in the other direction.

A third potential issue is a phenomenon described in an MIT paper called Representational Convergence. The paper suggests that over time, AI models from different companies all start to converge onto a similar statistical model of reality. If that’s true, then in five years Internet Court’s AI jurors could end up with a severe case of groupthink.

Castellana is not convinced this theory is valid. “I see many more models existing rather than fewer,” he says. “I see endless models coming from every country, every company, and every team. I cannot imagine that all of them will just converge into the exact same thing.”

#5066. North Korea Is Laundering Binance Hack Loot at One New Wallet Every 25 Seconds

North Korea Is Laundering Binance Hack Loot at One New Wallet Every 25 Seconds

North Korean hackers are moving the $6.4 billion they stole from Binance at a blistering clip: roughly one new crypto wallet every 25 seconds.

In under four days, the attacker has spread the funds across about 12,000 wallets on 11 blockchains in more than 40,000 transactions, according to an analysis published Monday by blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

But stablecoin issuers moved even faster. About $2.85 billion, or 44% of the haul, has been frozen or seized, compared with roughly 3% after North Korea’s $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025.

Speed mattered because stablecoins made up $3.46 billion, or 54%, of the haul, and their issuers can freeze them. Tether froze the stolen USDT on Ethereum 37 minutes after it reached the attacker’s wallet, and Circle froze all $823 million in stolen USDC before any of it could be swapped, Elliptic found.

Before the freezes landed, the hacker swapped 434 million USDT for ETH on decentralized exchanges such as Uniswap, borrowed 19,762 ETH on lending protocol Aave against another 59.9 million USDT, and traded 234 million USDT for Tron’s TRX token. In all, about $786 million in stablecoins got away.

Attackers began draining Binance’s hot wallets late Thursday. The FBI has since attributed the theft to TraderTraitor, a North Korean state-linked hacking group, and published hundreds of linked addresses.

Binance, which has pledged to cover all losses, reopened withdrawals Sunday and saw about $3.64 billion in net outflows in the first nine and a half hours.

The laundering that followed has been relentless. On Tron, which became the main route, activity peaked at about 1,300 transactions in a single hour.

That pace fueled speculation that AI agents were doing the work. Elliptic disagreed, pointing to fixed timing, set amounts, blind retries and pauses that resemble human working hours. The firm wrote that while the laundering is clearly automated, “nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions.”

About $3.24 billion remains in addresses linked to the theft, mostly ETH and the 18,270 stolen Bitcoin, which haven’t moved since the day of the attack. Elliptic believes those funds are very likely still held by North Korea, while money already being laundered may have passed to Chinese money-laundering networks.

Only a negligible amount appears to have been cashed out. About $16 million has also flowed to addresses holding proceeds of September’s Bitget exploit.

Based on the Bybit playbook, Elliptic expects the attacker to swap its ETH for Bitcoin in small batches and cash out through over-the-counter brokers over a period of months. Further freezes will be harder, the firm said, since ETH and Bitcoin can’t be frozen. The best remaining chances lie with centralized services, where the funds will eventually have to surface.

The Binance theft caps a punishing stretch for crypto security. BTCPay Server urged merchants to switch off their servers amid reports of hot wallet attacks on Monday, and Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin halted withdrawals after a $40 million hack. It’s unclear whether either incident is connected to the Binance attack.

#5063. The 2026 Unofficial Marathon Awards, According to an Editor Running New York City

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

This is the gear you actually need to make it to 26.2.

Last year, when I trained for my first marathon, I went a bit gear-crazy. I searched high and low for all the best products to help me get through training and across the finish line. And, I had a lot of fun doing it. I tested out dozens of sneakers, agonized over my race day outfit, and indulged in all the high-tech recovery gadgets (I have a recovery tool for basically every part of my body, from head to toe).

This year, my approach to gear is a lot simpler because I know what I like. More importantly, I know what works. For example: I’m going on two training cycles now of exclusively using Gu Raspberry Lemonade gels as my fuel on long runs. Yeah, when something is good, I’m dedicated.

So, while some of my training staple top spots haven’t budged—like my Bombas running socks, Garmin Forerunner 570, and Therabody JetBoots Pro Plus—that just means the things in this year’s Marathon Awards really impressed me.

I’m still loyal to many of the products in last year’s roundup of my faves, but these are the new additions that deserve a spot on the list.

I have been pretty dead-set on my Nike Vomero Plus (which I wore throughout training and on race day last year) as my max cushioned shoe for everyday runs for a while—but these sneaks from Hoka finally gave them some competition for that spot. They’re less pillowy and more structured, so I feel more in control on a run, and my heel and ankle have more support. During training this time around, I found that my feet started to crave a bit more of that stability from a shoe, so I switched over to the Cliftons to get me through my peak mileage. They hold their own on easy and long runs when it comes to comfort, and feel even better than the Nikes when I’m doing my speed intervals on the track.

There comes a point in training when I simply cannot get myself to wear anything but sneakers. My feet and body just need the support, even at work or running errands or going out with friends. The new Eliot Ryder from Tracksmith is a pillowy soft shoe that cushions every step. Made for running, it could totally double as your easy run go-to, but I’ve been living in it outside of logging miles as well. The shoe’s design also makes it a little more fashionable than other max-cushioned trainers that scream “running shoe.”

Recovery slides are definitely new for my training this year. After dealing with some mysterious foot aches and pains this time around, I started to wear slides around my apartment to avoid walking around flat-footed. Compared to others I tried, these are the softest and offer the most arch support.

I ran my favorite half marathon to date (the Lululemon SeaWheeze half) in this tank—so I’m a little sentimental. But still: it has the classic soft and breathable material I love Lululemon for, it’s super comfortable and lightweight over long distances, and comes in bright colors that will get you spotted on the course. I’m very much considering ironing my name onto the one I have and wearing it on race day.

I trained during the summer months in New York City, so a lightweight top was critical. This one from Salomon kept me comfortable throughout training (and also has a really cool, elevated look). I cannot stress enough just how lightweight, breathable, and quick-drying this fabric is. Truly a gem of a find!

Now that the weather is cooling down, this top from Rhone is my favorite for an early fall day. The material is soft and breathable, making it a perfect transition layer. And, I think the crop and mesh design make it cuter than other running long sleeves.

I would say the biggest thing I didn’t nail down last year was a running vest, so I’m excited to share that I’ve got one that I loved this time around. The big problem with last year’s (unnamed) vest is that the water bottles were always popping out. They stay put in this one from Hoka, which also feels lightweight and easily stores everything I need to bring with me. The closures are easy to work, it feels really secure and comfortable across long distances, and I like the pop the purple gives my usually all-black running outfit.

When all my laundry is clean, these are the first pair of shorts I grab above every other option. The main reason: I want to be comfortable on a run, and these are the most comfortable shorts I have by far. They don’t ride up, provide compression—but not too much compression—and have a softer feel. They have two pockets along the back (one zippered, where I keep my keys) and a mesh one (where I throw my empty gels), plus one on the right thigh (where I keep my phone).

This pair from Bandit also hits all the important notes in running shorts: they don’t ride up, offer compression, and are comfortable even on double-digit mile runs. These shorts also have extra grippy material inside to help them stay put and have pockets on both thighs and two along the back center, which is helpful if you have more things to carry with you on a run. (Come race day, when I don’t plan to wear a vest because I won’t need to carry my own water supply, I’m going to be happy to have an extra pocket to hold my gels!)

Lululemon’s low-impact Flow Y bra got an upgrade to be more supportive for higher-impact activities. The racerback bra has a mesh panel along the back too, making it more breathable on hot days. While training through the summer, I mostly end up running in just a sports bra, and this one also has a fun pattern to show off.

It’s very hard to successfully cross a finish line without the help of a training program. This year, I worked with Kelly Roberts, an RRCA-certified run coach and creator of the Badass Lady Gang. It’s a run community, and now, an app where you can get customized training programs, connect with Kelly and other runners, and utilize strength training, fueling, mindset, and recovery resources. It’s the most thorough platform I’ve ever seen, and Kelly was there to help me navigate the highs (5 and 10K PRs when I wasn’t even trying) and lows (strep throat) that popped up during training.

Bose just dropped these earbuds and I’ve been obsessed ever since I got my hands on them. They stay secure on your ears and have that open ear feature so you can still hear everything around you. But, since they’re Bose, the sound also feels more high quality than other options, and they’re slightly louder for moments when you really need to pump those tunes.

I love these sunglasses from Warby Parker Sport. They’re lightweight, but still feel like a solid, nice pair of sunglasses, and their look feels a little more elevated than some of the aggressively big and bold sunglasses that runners have to choose from. They also stay on my face, rain, shine, or sweat.

Not to be dramatic, but these hair ties from Goody changed my life. Last year, I watched a bunch of tutorials of how different influencers styled their hair for a race and purchased different products that would hopefully help tame my flyaways and often-drooping ponytail to no avail. It turns out, all I needed were these hair ties. They’re thicker and stronger than the regular old ones I’ve been wearing since I was a kid, and seem impossible to break. I can run for miles now and my hair doesn’t budge. I don’t know what kind of magic is in these things, but I’m grateful.

I don’t love the way most sunscreens sit on my skin, but obviously, some SPF is important when you’re spending that much time in the sun. I actually like this sunscreen from Summer Fridays—it melts into my skin, keeps me covered, and doesn’t get into my eyes when I’m on a run.

If you’re acne-prone like me, all that running and sweating that comes with marathon training could do a number on your skin. This year, I got in the habit of immediately rinsing my face with water once I got back to my apartment and then spraying it with Tower 28’s SOS spray for good measure. So far, no major breakouts to report.

Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. #5061. Strategy spends triple its weekly Bitcoin outlay on STRC buybacks

Strategy spent $208.2 million buying back STRC preferred shares and $65.5 million on Bitcoin, while allocating $98.5 million to its USD Cash balance.

Michael Saylor’s Strategy spent more than three times as much buying back its STRC preferred shares as it did acquiring Bitcoin last week.

Strategy repurchased $208.2 million in STRC and spent about $65.5 million buying 811 Bitcoin (BTC) at an average price of $80,846, according to a Monday 8-K filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Strategy sold about 1.18 million MSTR shares for net proceeds of $164 million. About $65.5 million funded its Bitcoin acquisition, while the remaining $98.5 million was allocated to the company’s USD Cash balance, according to the filing. The company held $132.7 million in USD Cash and $4.79 billion in its separate US dollar reserve as of Sunday.

The latest purchases followed about $664 million in STRC buybacks between Oct. 1 and Oct. 25, compared with about $72 million in Bitcoin acquisitions over that period.

Strategy’s STRC preferred stock rose 0.1% in pre-market trading on Monday, to $99.22, or about 0.8% below its $100 target price, according to Yahoo Finance. Strategy’s policy is not to issue new STRC shares below $100, limiting that source of funding while the stock trades below its target.

Another Bitcoin treasury company, Japan’s Metaplanet, reported buybacks of its common shares on Monday. It spent about 25.27 billion yen ($160 million) to buy back 96.45 million shares between Oct. 14 and Saturday, representing roughly 7.1% of its issued shares.

#5059. Tom Lee Calls Bottom on AI Stock Selloff, Citing Tight Supply and Crypto Resilience

Tom Lee Calls Bottom on AI Stock Selloff, Citing Tight Supply and Crypto Resilience

Fundstrat co-founder and Bitmine chairman Tom Lee says the AI stock selloff has reached its bottom, arguing that demand remains constrained by supply and that crypto’s response to a multibillion-dollar hack suggests investors have already done much of their selling.

“I think we're at the bottom,” Lee said in a CNBC interview on Squawk Box. His call follows a decline that has spread beyond individual AI companies: large technology stocks also sit inside the funds many people use to invest for retirement. (Disclosure: Tom Lee is one of several investors in Dastan, Decrypt’s parent company.)

Lee acknowledged how difficult it is to identify the point where prices stop falling. “Bottoms are dangerous because you don't know how much more downside you have at the bottom,” he said.

His case rests on a distinction between an industry struggling to expand fast enough and one that has built more capacity than customers need.

“The market for AI is still very tight. It's not an overcapacity market,” Lee said. Constraints on spending, including political opposition, are slowing growth without signaling that the boom has peaked, he argued.

The distinction matters for companies building the infrastructure behind AI, including data centers, the facilities that house the computers running these systems. A shortage can leave customers waiting, but it can also prevent suppliers from turning orders into revenue.

Intel offered a particularly sharp example on Friday, when its shares plunged nearly 30% to $65.76 after a disappointing fourth-quarter forecast. Its data center and AI revenue had risen 78% from a year earlier to $7.3 billion, yet manufacturing constraints limited its ability to meet demand.

Intel projected quarterly revenue of $15 billion to $16 billion, with the midpoint about $1.6 billion below analysts’ expectations. Its weaker PC business added pressure, showing why healthy AI demand can coexist with a falling share price.

Lee’s second argument looks outside the stock market. He pointed to crypto’s relatively limited response to the Binance security breach, which the exchange initially estimated had an impact of about $6.3 billion.

“If crypto is a leading indicator for risk, you've already seen people sort of washed out,” he said. In his reading, a market that absorbs bad news without a much larger decline may have fewer sellers left to push prices down.

Bitcoin did fall about 4% to roughly $76,400 on Friday, and more than $1.3 billion in positions funded with borrowed money were forcibly closed. U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds, which hold Bitcoin and trade like stocks, also shed $643.2 million that day, their largest daily outflow of October.

That said, a 4% drop can happen even during bull runs and it is considered a relatively normal occurrence among crypto traders.

Binance said on Friday that about $3 billion of the affected assets had been frozen and pledged to cover losses using its emergency reserve and company funds.

#5058. Animoca Brands clears final hurdle for Abu Dhabi fund management

The former mobile games company is building out its fund management business in Abu Dhabi as it targets institutional demand for stablecoins and tokenized assets.

Animoca Brands said Monday it had secured approval to manage investment funds for non-retail clients in Abu Dhabi, expanding the asset management business of a company that began in mobile games before moving into blockchain gaming and venture investing.

The authorization, granted to subsidiary Animoca Asset Management (AD) Limited by Abu Dhabi Global Market’s Financial Services Regulatory Authority (FSRA), allows it to oversee funds that pool money from professional investors rather than the general public.

The approval announced Monday took effect on Oct. 13, bringing a process that began with in-principle approval in November 2025 to completion. In the intervening months, Animoca also secured a Dubai license in February to provide virtual asset brokerage and investment management services to institutional and qualified investors.

Animoca Brands was spun off from mobile game developer Animoca in 2014, initially developing and publishing games based on licensed brands alongside original titles. It shifted its focus toward blockchain in 2018 and built an investment portfolio that included OpenSea, Dapper Labs and Axie Infinity developer Sky Mavis.

In a September investor update, the company described a broader transition from a digital asset and Web3 operator into a regulated financial services institution operating across multiple jurisdictions, amid a broader push by crypto-native companies into traditional financial services. Under that strategy, digital assets are taking on a more institutional role in its business, with a focus on stablecoins, real-world asset tokenization and treasury management.

#5056. The 10 Best Hiking Backpacks, Tested by Outdoor Gear Editors

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

We tested dozens of packs and found Osprey’s Kestrel LT 45 to be the best.

A GOOD HIKING backpack is just as important—if not more so—as a good pair of hiking boots, hiking shoes, or any other hiking gear. Whether you're venturing deep into the backcountry or exploring a local trail, your pack will be strapped to your back for an extended period of time. It needs to be reliable, comfortable, durable, and offer the right storage and organization options to suit your style of hiking. Oh, and affordability is a nice-to-have, too.

Finding the right backpack for you can be like shopping for the perfect mattress. Backpack technology isn't what it used to be when your grandpappy was hitting the trail. It's far better but also a lot more complicated. You'll need to understand frame types and nylon fabric densities and hydration system compatibilities and—well, you get the picture. It's all just a little more confusing than it needs to be. That's why our expert team put together this guide to the best hiking backpacks to help you find the perfect pack without breaking your back and the bank in the process.

As an outdoors writer, I spend countless weeks outside each year. During that time, I've put the most popular and well-reviewed hiking backpacks to the test to determine the best of the best. Over the last 12 months, I've tested dozens of packs from Mystery Ranch, Gossamer Gear, Gregory, Osprey, and more throughout the U.S., in Utah's national parks, the White Mountains of New Hampshire, the vast deserts of Big Bend National Park, and beyond. I also consulted with Senior Gear and Reviews Editor Ryan Brower on top options, research, and some of his own personal testing. While testing hiking backpacks, I've been sure to take thorough notes on comfort, ease of packing, weight, durability, how well (or not) the weight is spread throughout, and how much gear can fit in them.

So, whether you're looking for a featherweight daypack for afternoon peak-bagging or a full-fledged expedition pack for tackling the wilds of Patagonia, we've got you covered with the 10 best hiking backpacks for any trail.

Winner of this year’s “Best New All-Purpose Backpack” in the latest Men’s Health Outdoor Awards, the Kestrel LT 45 is the bag to buy if you only want to buy one. It’s Osprey’s reimagining of the classic rucksack design. It’s rugged yet lightweight, with a 45-liter capacity that’s more than enough for most hikers on all but the longest multiday treks. I personally love the streamlined exterior that cinches down tight for maximum compression. Osprey’s AirScape back panel offers just-right cushioning, coupled with simple torso-length adjustability to fit a wide range of body shapes and sizes.

After months of testing, this has consistently proven to be my go-to pack for most hikes because everything about it just works. Plus, the price point hits the midrange sweet spot, ensuring it’s affordable enough for most, even for first-time hiking backpack buyers on a budget. Bonus: An attached rain cover is included.

At just 12 liters and weighing only 390 grams, this pack is designed for ultra-efficient climbs and lightning-fast summit assaults. It's the daypack you grab when most other daypacks are too much. I found the simple organizational layout—one main compartment and two zippered pockets (one internal, one external)—made wrangling my day hiking essentials a breeze. Additional gear loops at the rear provide extra space for hanging anything you might need at the ready. The just-right back padding, removable hip belt, and ventilated mesh shoulder straps were extremely comfy for tackling daily summits in the White Mountains.

It's all made from recycled, eco-friendly materials that are Bluesign certified, with a PFC-free DWR treatment. The best part? It's among the most affordable brand-name speedpacks we'd recommend this year.

Like every Gregory pack I've tested in the last two decades, the Paragon 60 is durable, cleverly designed, and features smart organizational options. But I'm including it here mainly because it's so dang comfy to wear. It features a widely adjustable torso length and an airy mesh back panel that breathes well thanks to a design that's 90 percent air. The hip belt is equally adjustable, too, with dynamic panels that move independently of the frame. Translation: It's the most comfortable pack I tested this year, even after hundreds of miles of hiking with fairly heavy loads.

Bonus: The PFAS-free durable water-repellent (DWR) coating on the body, bottom, and pack lining means it's more eco-friendly, too.

Yeti’s first foray into the hardcore hiking backpack game comes on the heels of its recent acquisition of the Mystery Ranch brand. The Skala is a deft combination of Mystery Ranch’s legendary design prowess and Yeti’s built-to-survive-anything durability. The former’s RipZip opening is the star of this pack, providing instant access to its contents with a quick one-handed pull. The Skala features multiple adjustment points and a clever load distribution system that combine to ensure a perfect fit and all-day comfort on the trail.

True to the Yeti brand, it's packed with premium, heavy-duty materials built to survive years of abuse. The only downsides? At 3.4 pounds, it’s heavier than many in this weight class. It’s also pricier than most of its competition. But if you’re a fan of Yeti and/or MR and don’t mind the “buy once, cry once” price point, this is definitely worth considering.

For an all-in-one solution—a backpack that moves effortlessly from your morning commute to the office to the trail and back again—Peak Design's new Outdoor 25L pack delivers. The frameless, low-profile build is extremely comfy on and off the trail. The brand's signature organizational elements are here, too—it's hydration system-compatible, features a 16-inch laptop sleeve, and is purpose-built to accept PD's own gear cubes (sold separately) so you can divvy up the main compartment however you see fit. The drawstring/roll-top closure provides quick and easy access to the internal compartment while protecting your gear from the elements. Fair warning, though: You'll pay a premium for the PD brand name. If you’re looking to size up, the Outdoor Backpack is also available as a more generous 45-liter option.

Mountain Hardwear is upending the ultralight backpacking game with the debut of the all-new Alakazam series. The 45-liter model won a coveted spot in this year’s Men’s Health Outdoor Awards. At just 820 grams, it’s absurdly lightweight with a trail-friendly fit that feels like wearing nothing at all. Yet, it offers enough room for both gear-heavy day hikes and overnights. It’s all thanks to the bleeding-edge ALUULA shell fabric, which is not only light but extremely durable. The storage and organization (with plenty of loops, pockets, and oversized stash pouches) are cleverly laid out, and the unique GiddyUp compression system allows you to cinch your load down on the go. I also especially love the roll-top design that makes the entire bag waterproof. (A 60-liter option is also available.)

Like many Arc'teryx packs, the Alpha FL30 is first and foremost built for climbing (including a top strap, six lash points, and a built-in RECCO reflector for SAR situations). But, I also found its sleek silhouette and featherweight construction great for fast-packing. It's only as heavy as it needs to be while still protecting your gear with the brand's own abrasion-resistant Hadron grid fabric. The drawstring opening provides quick access to a cavernous, open-concept interior with only a single, small zippered security pocket.

It's a no-frills design with minimal organization options that won't please every hiker. But it's a solid, if pricey, option for hard-charging trail lovers who appreciate a pack that stays out of its own way.

With a 44-liter capacity, the Talon 44 is large enough for two-day weekender trips and even longer multiday treks. It's an ideal bag for most hikers looking to buy just one backpack that does it all. I love the slim profile, excellent padding, and close-to-body fit—all of which makes for more comfortable wear, especially after long days on the trail.

This iteration of Osprey's best-seller features a long list of upgrades to the straps, padding, and overall design. Plus, I found the sheer number of storage options to be perfect for almost any length of trip.

I’ve been a fan of Gossamer Gear’s featherweight fastpacker-friendly gear for years. The Grit 28 is my go-to for fast day hikes and quick summit assaults where I’m willing to trade comfort and convenience for speed. At just over a pound, the 28-liter pack is streamlined and sized right. The storage options are limited, ensuring you pack only what you need and nothing more.

What sets it apart from most of its competition is the vest-style shoulder and sternum straps that wear more like a racing vest for maximum all-day comfort. Despite its light weight, it’s made from two ultra-durable fabrics that I’ve found stand up to plenty of trail abuse.

The Crossroads 27L Carry-On is, first and foremost, a travel backpack (it fits under most airline seats, making it a great carry-on backpack). But Yeti bakes in plenty of trail-ready durability to make this one good enough for hiking, too. The 700-denier TuffSkin Nylon exterior is both water- and abrasion-resistant, with an extra durable and easy-to-clean PU-coated base. Contoured, EVA-foam shoulder straps and a no-pressure back panel make for easy carrying, even during long layovers.

Plus, with a suspended laptop sleeve and multiple zippered pockets inside and out, this has become my go-to weekender bag, especially on trips where I'm intent on hitting the trail straight from the airport. (Pro tip: Pair it with Yeti's own Crossroads packing cubes for maximum organization options.)

Ultimately, every hiking backpack is designed for hauling "stuff." The amount of stuff each pack can carry—its capacity—is measured in liters (L) or cubic inches. The right size for you depends on how long you typically hike for (hours, days, weeks?) and how heavy a packer you are. If you're shopping for your first pack, finding the perfect capacity can involve a little trial and error. But for a wide variety of hikers, the following numbers are a good starting point.

If you tend to pack light, the right capacity for you may fall closer to the lower end of the above ranges. If you're an over-packer (no shame!), consider sizing up to a slightly larger pack with a capacity on the upper end of the above ranges. Again: Trial and error.

You may have heard an old hiker's adage that goes something like, "Ounces make pounds, and pounds make pain." It's a simple axiom, but it's true: The more weight you carry, the more work you need to do on the trail. That's why it's important to pack as light as possible while still taking all the essentials. It helps to start with as light a hiking backpack as you can afford. The difference between three pounds and four pounds might not seem like much when you're standing in your living room, but I promise you'll feel every gram of that one-pound difference after 10 miles.

There are three main types of backpack construction: internal frame, external frame, and frameless. External frame packs have largely been phased out in the mainstream outdoor gear space in favor of internal frame models. True to the name, they feature a rigid structure that's "hidden" inside the pack's back panel. They sit closer to the back, hugging the body for a more streamlined fit that's ultimately more comfortable. All but the smallest hiking backpacks we recommend below rely on an internal frame construction.

Some compact daypacks (usually 20 liters or less) use a frameless construction similar to standard school backpacks. The lack of a frame means they're not ideal for supporting heavier loads, but that typically isn't an issue if you're only carrying enough for an afternoon hike. Without a frame, most of these packs collapse down almost completely flat, making them perfect travel backpacks.

Some packs—particularly those designed for alpine climbers—rely on top-loading-only designs. Think of a typical shopping bag where the contents are only accessible directly through the top opening. It's simple but limiting. Thankfully, many of the best hiking backpacks these days are accessible via both top and side openings. For larger packs, this makes it infinitely easier to get to the gear at the bottom of your pack without having to take everything out first.

The "best" storage layout is personal to each hiker. If you're an organizational nerd (I count myself among this camp), you may prefer more pockets to better wrangle your gear in a way that makes sense to you. On the other end of the spectrum are alpine-style bags that typically trade more pockets (and zippers and stitching) for a simpler, lighter-weight design. These tend to feature a single open-concept storage compartment with only one or two smaller storage pockets.

A few pieces of gear you want to pack in your hiking backpack include: a hydration bladder, water bottle(s), a phone (with easy access to its camera), a satellite messenger, and bear spray. Most modern bags also offer external storage options, like reinforced crampon patches and gear loops or "daisy chains" (i.e., webbing stitched into the pack's exterior that provides multiple attachment points for wet gear, ice axes, helmets, and more that you may not want to store inside your pack).

Modern hiking backpacks can be deceptively complex, and there are dozens of technical features available on many of today's best models. If you're shopping for anything other than a daypack, consider a bag with a dedicated sleeping bag compartment and sleeping pad straps. For longer treks where you may want to venture away from base camp once you set up your tent, a removable daypack (sometimes the "brain" or top lid of a larger pack converts into an ultra-compact hip bag for this purpose), and a rain cover, which may be a stand-alone piece of fabric with a stretch seam or sewn into the pack itself, so you can easily pull it over the pack if the weather turns south.

Mike Richard has traveled the world since 2008. He's kayaked in Antarctica, tracked endangered African wild dogs in South Africa, and survived a near-miss great white shark attack in Mexico. His travel advice has appeared on the websites for Forbes, Travel + Leisure, CNET, and National Geographic. He loves the great outdoors and good bourbon, and (usually) calls Tulsa, Oklahoma home. Mike also enjoys speaking in the third person.

Ryan Brower is the Senior Gear and Reviews Editor at Men’s Health, where he tests the best products in outdoor gear, tech, running shoes, and more. He has written about the outdoors for nearly two decades and was previously the Managing Editor, Content Performance at Gear Patrol. He holds a B.A. in Sport Management and is a Certified Beer Server, having previously covered the craft beer industry. Ryan currently lives on the water in New Jersey with his wife and their dog.

#5053. Mercado Bitcoin wallets show post-hack transfers after $46M theft

Brazil’s exchange Mercado Bitcoin paused deposits and withdrawals after a security breach, saying customer funds and data were safe.

Mercado Bitcoin, one of Brazil’s largest cryptocurrency exchanges, confirmed a security incident on Monday that blockchain analysts estimated drained more than $46 million from its wallets. Despite the company’s announcement that it had suspended deposits and withdrawals, addresses associated with the incident continued to receive funds for hours afterward.

Senior security engineer at Certik, Dickson Wu, published an initial post-mortem of the incident, saying the attackers “picked at eight small chains for four hours,” including Base, Optimism and Arbitrum. The attack then moved to the company’s main hot wallets before transferring tiny amounts of Ether (ETH) from 27,160 different addresses.

“So whoever did this can also move money out of MB’s customer deposit addresses,” he wrote.

Security firms have stated the hack was carried out through standard transfers rather than a smart contract exploit. Mercado Bitcoin said the breach would have no financial impact on customers.

According to Wu, hours after Mercado Bitcoin announced a temporary suspension of deposits and withdrawals, funds continued to move out of addresses tied to the breach, followed by thousands of small ETH transfers into a single address.

A spokesperson for Mercado Bitcoin told Cointelegraph that customers had suffered no losses and no data had been leaked, but the company did not respond to requests for comment on the continued transfers. A data breach could also trigger notification requirements to Brazil’s data protection authority.

The hack has not yet been attributed to an organized group and remains under investigation.

Of the stolen funds, by 4:42 pm UTC approximately $6.2 million had been frozen by stablecoin issuers Tether and Circle. About $20 million had been transferred into newly created wallets, while over $20 million remained in associated addresses identified by Mercado Bitcoin, including all 106 BTC.

Mercado Bitcoin had 4.5 million users as of July, when stablecoin issuer Tether announced a $20 million investment to support its expansion in tokenization, payments and credit. Another Tether-backed exchange in Latin America, Chile’s Orionx, announced its closure in September after a forensic audit found that more than $7 million in customer assets had moved to wallets outside its control.

The incident comes days after Binance reported a $6.3 billion hack targeting its hot wallets on Thursday. The exchange suspended deposits and withdrawals and pledged to cover customer losses. Binance began resuming withdrawals on Sunday, saying it had replenished 95% of the stolen assets.

#5050. Claude-Built Linux Exploit Targets Bug That Ubuntu Still Hasn't Patched

Claude-Built Linux Exploit Targets Bug That Ubuntu Still Hasn't Patched

Another day, another exploit from ChatGPT or Claude that gives us the chills.

Anthropic says its AI wrote a working attack that lets a stranger take full control of a Linux server over the internet, no password needed. The bug behind it, CVE-2026-98432, has been hiding in Linux since version 4.4 and scores 9.8 out of 10 for severity.

Linux runs countless servers, and Ubuntu is one of its most popular flavors. Ubuntu's own tracker still lists every version from 16.04 to 26.10 as vulnerable.

Anthropic described the attack on Oct. 27 in a research post covering five targets: Chrome, Safari, Android, Windows, and Linux. Claude wrote the Linux exploit after initial prompting, using an unreleased internal model.

The attack takes less than an hour to find a hidden spot in the computer's memory, then five more minutes to run a command with the highest level of access, known as root.

The flaw works like reading a sticky note after someone already shredded it. One part of the system throws away a piece of information while another part is still using it, per AlmaLinux. A specially crafted message that opens an internet connection can set that off.

Failing costs the attacker almost nothing. "An enabling property of this race condition is that a lost race only fails the handshake without crashing the whole system," Anthropic wrote, "so a determined attacker can retry indefinitely until they succeed."

Anthropic's version needs a server using a feature called multipath TCP, which lets one connection travel over several routes at once. Programs written in Go have switched it on by default since Go 1.24, per the Go Security Team.

But the bug is not limited to that feature. "While the demonstrated exploit requires the target system to expose an MPTCP listener, the underlying bug is not specific to MPTCP," the Go Security Team wrote.

Red Hat released fixes for RHEL 8, 9 and 10 on Sunday, including RHSA-2026:93412. Debian, SUSE, Oracle Linux and Amazon Linux have also shipped fixes, at least for some versions.

For anyone who can't update yet, Red Hat published a temporary firewall rule. It "reduces exposure to this vulnerability but cannot fully prevent it," Red Hat says in its security bulletin. A full working solution is still under development.

Anthropic's earlier Claude Mythos model helped Mozilla find 271 bugs in Firefox, as Decrypt reported in April.

Ubuntu's tracker shows fixes in progress for 22.04, 24.04, 26.04 and 26.10. Versions 20.04, 18.04 and 16.04 show only "Vulnerable," and the tracker gives no release date for any fix.

#5049. 7 Best Shaving Creams for Men in 2026, According to a Grooming Expert

Say goodbye to razor burn and hello to a better shave.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

UNLESS YOU'RE SHAVING with a top-notch wet or dry electric razor, you’ll need to first apply a product to prep the skin. There are really only two options: pre-shave oil and shaving cream. The truth is, your shave is only as good as the cream you use because the right shaving cream cushions the skin, reduces irritation, and locks in moisture for added comfort afterwards. It also helps the razor glide smoothly, preventing nicks and tugging.

Most importantly, it turns a tedious routine into a clean, refreshing experience. But not all shaving creams are created equal—some barely protect or soothe at all. To save your skin the abuse (and potential scars), I’ve rounded up the best options worth trying.

The scent is invigorating and woodsy, thanks to natural oils like cedarwood, vetiver, and chamomile instead of synthetic fragrances. This also makes it safe for sensitive skin without being entirely scentless.

On top of its long list of at least 70 percent organic ingredients, it even comes in sustainable packaging made from recycled materials. I generally shave with a safety razor for my face and a cartridge razor for my body (or when traveling), and Jackfir was perfect for both.

It’s slick enough that it never clogs up the blade, but thick enough to keep my skin protected. This is all on top of prepping the skin while also locking moisture in post-shave.

I have dry skin that’s prone to shaving irritation, but the combo of panthenol and bisabolol kept my face feeling calm before and after the session. You also don’t need to use much to cover your entire face; barely more than a nickel-sized amount will do the trick. The only downside to this stuff is that it’s over $35, but given how long it lasts and how great it performs, it’s well worth it.

The razor you use isn’t solely responsible for shaving irritation. For many, simply switching to a gentler, more skin-friendly shaving cream will go a long way in preventing bumps and redness. You won’t find a better option than Harry’s Shave Cream, which is no surprise coming from what I dubbed the best skincare brand for clean-shaven men.

This cream packs powerful natural ingredients like eucalyptus oil, mint oil, and aloe vera, which work together to soothe skin and reduce irritation. It’s free of parabens, sulfates, or other common irritants, and its formula is equally thick and silky to protect and smooth skin. Best of all, it could double as the best value shaving cream at only $8 a container.

I’ve been cutting my own hair for over a decade, so I really only go to barbershops for one thing: a close and luxurious shave. But this shaving cream makes me wonder if I could eliminate those trips altogether. It’s the closest you’ll get to the barbershop experience with an incredible, soft lather that feels like a hot towel wrapped around your chin. Sure, it’s a little pricey and best applied with a brush—which I count as a positive—but it’s far less expensive and time-consuming than making a trip to your local shop.

If you’re prone to razor bumps and burning like me, this cream will feel like a godsend thanks to its rich and smooth lather that’s packed with ingredients that care for skin. The aloe vera and shea butter deliver deep hydration, keeping your face calm and protected while helping to prevent razor bumps and irritation.

But if I’m traveling or in a hurry, I’ll turn to them—and rarely without this shave gel from GilletteLabs. The rapid foaming is as advertised, thickening in seconds for quick application.

It gives that fresh feeling once it hits your face before the razor even touches your skin. While it’s listed as having a cedar wood and orange peel scent, I rarely catch too much of it. But I prefer that, as I’d rather rely on my cologne for fragrance.

I implore you to use a shave oil in addition to a cream to minimize irritation as much as possible; however, if you really don’t have the time or desire to, use this. The lotion-like texture takes a couple of shaves to get used to if you’ve traditionally used thicker shave creams or foams, but the feel of your skin during and after is immediately satisfying.

Shaving cream is often used as a catch-all term for several products that prep and protect your skin while you shave. There are three main types, and the right one depends on your razor, your skin type, and your personal preference.

If your shave is only as good as the cream you use, then the cream is only as good as its ingredients. Shaving creams often include soothing ingredients like aloe vera, which calms irritation and helps the skin stay hydrated before and after shaving. Shea butter is another popular one, as it provides rich moisture that softens hair and improves razor glide, reducing friction during the shave.

Many formulas also contain vitamin E, an antioxidant that nourishes the skin and supports repair after shaving. Then there is a slew of natural oils and other vitamins to keep an eye on, like vitamin B5, vitamin C, argan oil, and coconut oil that hydrate, soothe, and prevent post-shave irritation.

How often do you shave? That’s the most important question to ask yourself when considering the size of your cream. A larger tube offers better value and lasts longer, especially for daily shavers. Smaller sizes, on the other hand, are ideal for travel or testing a new formula before committing. You’ll also want to consider what you shave.

I rock a beard most of the time, so I only shave for touch-ups on my neck and cheeks. Plus, I rarely shave body hair and stick to trimming. Because of this, I can often get away with a smaller tube. But if you’re clean-cut or shave large parts of your body, you’ll want to look to larger options.

#5043. It Just Got Easier to Get Peptides in the US. Here's What Could Come Next.

Spoiler: More access doesn’t necessarily mean more safety.

ON WEDNESDAY, THE U.S. Food and Drug Administration announced a new policy that makes six experimental peptides more readily available. According to a document released by the HHS, the government will not penalize specialized pharmacies for making and supplying these popular substances to patients with a prescription. The peptides: BPC-157, epitalon, KPV, MOTS-c, Semax, and TB-500.

This does not mean these particular peptides have been approved for use by the FDA. In fact, none of them have gone through the rigorous human testing required for FDA drug approval. And the new policy is not intended to be permanent. Instead, it allows temporary access as the FDA decides on a longer-term plan for the six peptides. It also includes the caveat that compounders report “serious adverse events, and product quality issues to FDA and to the States where the products are compounded,” according to the document.

This topic has been red-hot since July, when the FDA held an advisory committee meeting to consider whether to add these peptides to a list of substances that U.S. compounding pharmacies are allowed to manufacture and supply. These substances are already widely used and obtained on the gray market. If people are going to use them, advocates have argued, it would be safest if those substances came from licensed pharmacies and are used with physician oversight.

Critics, on the other hand—including FDA scientists—are concerned that the government may be opening the floodgates to under-studied and potentially harmful drugs. These substances, they note, have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness. And some of these peptides are already being promoted and sold on the basis of scientific claims that experts say are unsupported or questionable.

Here's what could come next, if you're thinking about taking one of these six compounded peptides.

Some parts of the current conversation played out at last month’s PepMed conference in Washington, D.C. A sign pointing guests to the registration table featured a stylized gold peptide overlaid on the white dome of the U.S. Capitol. PepMed’s organizer, the American Academy of Peptide Medicine (AAPM), founded just last year, has already become a major voice in this debate.

In advance of the FDA’s July meeting, AAPM submitted hundreds of pages to a crowded public docket, including information about each of the seven peptides then under review. Several of its advisory board members spoke during the meeting’s public comment sessions. And the group appears to have the ear of the administration. PepMed speakers included the assistant secretary for health, Admiral Brian Christine, and a host of prominent MAHA figures.

The September event opened with an on-the-record media hour, featuring a dozen speakers, including physicians and owners and CEOs of compounding pharmacies and telehealth companies. A recurring theme was the danger of the gray market, where peptide quality can be difficult or impossible to verify and consumers often make treatment decisions without medical oversight—“this is not a victory for patient safety,” said Ricardo Rossello, PhD, a U.S. congressional delegate for Puerto Rico and AAPM advisory board member who co-founded Beijing Prosperous Biopharm, a medical research company.

Many of the speakers also lamented the failure of the U.S. health care system to fully meet the needs of patients, and some of the day’s speakers, including Alex Tatem, MD, an Indiana-based urologist and men’s health specialist with a large YouTube following, presented experimental peptides as part of the solution. “Our established medical system is very much a sick care model,” said Tatem, who serves on the AAPM’s medical advisory board. It’s important to help patients in a health crisis, he told me in an interview. But people want to be proactive about disease prevention. In his view, the peptides under FDA’s consideration can play a role by expanding “the toolkit that’s available to providers.”

At the FDA’s July advisory committee meeting, the agency’s scientists advised against allowing broad access to these peptides, in part because they have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness in the kind of long-term, rigorously designed human studies typically required for drug approval.Even at the conference, some doctors acknowledged the evidence gaps. Abud Bakri, MD, works in California as a physician who specializes in caring for hospitalized patients. He also runs a separate medical practice focused on preventive and longevity medicine, and he serves on the Enhanced Games Independent Medical Commission. His interest in peptides has been long-standing enough to predate his medical career.

Bakri, now 33, has been a powerlifter since he was 17. He explained that he has a genetic condition that may increase his risk for ligament injuries. He first used BPC-157 almost a decade ago, and believes the peptide helped him recover faster, though he acknowledges that this is hard to prove definitively, given the absence of well-designed human studies.

If just a fraction of the rat and Petri dish research holds up in humans, BPC-157 could be an incredibly helpful drug in the ICU, he told me at the conference, during an interview at an outdoor dining area. But ideally, he says, its effectiveness should be proven before it’s used in this setting and potential side effects should be better understood. “If I’m going to have an honest conversation with a patient,” he said, “I need to be able to fully elucidate the risk, which I can’t for some of these compounds.”

After the conference, I spoke by Zoom with Thomas Kremen, MD, a physician at UCLA Health who works for several athletic teams, including the Los Angeles Chargers and Lakers. Kremen and his colleagues recently reviewed the evidence behind six popular experimental peptides. He and his co-authors seemed unimpressed. “It’s probably not a strong stance to be recommending these things,” he told me.

Would it be accurate for physicians to tell their patients that a peptide like BPC-157 heals tissue?

“Maybe if they're talking about a rat in some study,” he said. “But I think if you're making a claim, you better be able to cite the literature that supports that.”

Right now, not only is there a dearth of high-quality clinical evidence that peptides work and are safe, unsupported claims are being made about these compounds.

For instance, among the documents that AAPM submitted to the FDA’s public docket was a white paper claiming that two experimental peptides—epitalon and BPC-157—are endogenous, indicating they are naturally produced by the body. Similar descriptions can be found on websites for telehealth companies and online peptide vendors suggesting their products are synthetic versions of naturally derived peptides.

Epitalon is a lab-made peptide based on an extract that researchers in the Soviet Union obtained from cattle brains. Terry Roberts, PhD, a researcher who studies epitalon at Brunel University of London, said it’s unclear whether humans produce this peptide. As far as he’s aware, no studies have explicitly described its presence in humans.

And BPC-157? The peptide was discovered in gastric juice, said Vince Luca, PhD, a researcher at the Moffitt Cancer Center in Florida. Yet 35 years after its discovery, nobody has found a gene that encodes for it—it could have come from a microbe or from food. It’s not impossible that the body produces BPC-157, said Luca, but he thinks it’s unlikely. He also says that health care professionals “should know enough not to tell [people] that it's an endogenous peptide” when it is very unlikely to be one. And regardless, he continued, “there are plenty of endogenous peptides that, injected at high concentrations, would have a really negative effect on the body.” In other words, being endogenous alone doesn’t mean it’s safe.

(The American Academy of Peptide Medicine’s press team did not respond to queries asking how the group determined that BPC-157 and epitalon are endogenous.)

“My prediction is that those who are already using the gray market will continue to do so,” said Ryan Beattie, director of business development at Adaptog Research & Life Sciences, a UK-based company that sells experimental peptides. “So I think in the initial term, people will use compounding pharmacies in the US. What I then predict will happen is people will then get used to it, get more aware of it—and then switch to the gray market because of the cost.”

He may be right. For this story, we spoke with two peptide users who had been living with chronic pain, and after trying physical therapy as well as other treatment approaches, turned to the Wolverine stack, a combination of BPC-157 and TB-500. Both said they felt the peptides helped them—and neither was sure they’d switch to compounded versions, which are more expensive than the gray market and aren’t covered by insurance. One of them, Charles A., asked, “What are the hoops and what are the costs going to be?”

A third person, who asked to be referred to as James—the name he uses in online communities—tried an oral version of BPC-157 four years ago and said he hasn’t been the same since then. Shortly after taking the experimental compound, he felt anxiety followed by sensory and emotional numbness that still hasn’t gone away. He also developed an autoimmune condition. Online, he found others with some similar experiences. When he looked carefully at the published research, he concluded that it didn’t match the online hype. “I felt very foolish afterwards.”

Some scientists are continuing to investigate these peptides. At Moffitt, Luca is currently studying BPC-157 and he’s keeping an open mind.

Over the next few months, he and his colleagues plan to run a variety of very basic experiments in which they add the peptide to human cells and see how those cells respond. The results could provide additional insight into what, precisely, this molecule is doing in the body—and they may help explain why users have reported everything from striking benefits to lasting harm.

Perhaps one day, BPC-157 will prove to be an effective drug, said Luca. But for now, his advice is to wait on using it and other experimental peptides: “I just worry that people are a little too cavalier in how these are handled.”

Sara Talpos is a Michigan-based journalist covering biomedical research and public health. She’s written for publications including STAT, Science, and Undark, where she’s a contributing editor.

#5042. Crypto investigators flag suspected $106M theft, $786K Solana bot drain

A European entity may have lost $106 million from exchange accounts, while a wallet associated with a Solana arbitrage bot may have lost another $786,000.

An unidentified entity in the European Union may have lost more than $106 million from accounts at multiple cryptocurrency exchanges on Sunday, according to blockchain investigator ZachXBT.

In a Monday post, ZachXBT said funds suspected to have been stolen were withdrawn in USDC and USDT before being moved through several cryptocurrencies and blockchains.

The suspected attacker swapped USDC for Ether (ETH), used instant exchange services including WellWallet and Kyrrex, and converted funds into Monero (XMR) through the swap service Wagyu.

The suspected attacker remains unidentified, though ZachXBT said the movement of funds pointed away from a link to North Korea’s Lazarus Group. The affected entity and exchanges involved have not been publicly named.

ZachXBT identified 11 wallet addresses associated with the funds.

The reported theft follows the $6.3 billion Binance hack on Oct. 29, which the FBI has attributed to North Korean hackers known as “TraderTraitor,” a group linked to Lazarus. The attack was the largest cryptocurrency theft on record.

Related: Binance net outflows surge to $5.8B as withdrawals resume after record hack

A wallet associated with King, an automated trading bot on Solana, may have lost about $786,000 on Monday, according to pseudonymous blockchain monitoring account Onchain Lens.

King is an arbitrage bot, a software that automatically buys cryptocurrency in one market and sells it in another to profit from price differences. Onchain Lens told Cointelegraph the funds moved through several Solana wallets before being transferred to Ethereum using Mayan, a service for moving assets between blockchains. It said the rapid transfers suggested an attempt to conceal the funds’ origin and a possible wallet compromise.

King’s operator has not confirmed a compromise.

“Our report is based on unusual fund movements, rapid transfers and the subsequent cross-chain movement of funds, which we believe indicates an attempt to obscure their origin,” Onchain Lens told Cointelegraph. “This pattern strongly suggests a potential wallet compromise.”

#5041. Mystery $106 Million Crypto Theft Hits EU Entity Days After Binance Hack

Mystery $106 Million Crypto Theft Hits EU Entity Days After Binance Hack

Another nine-figure theft has hit crypto, just days after the biggest heist in the industry’s history.

An unnamed entity in the European Union is suspected of losing more than $106 million from several of its accounts on centralized exchanges on Sunday, pseudonymous onchain investigator ZachXBT said Monday in a post to his Telegram channel.

The funds were withdrawn as USDC and USDT stablecoins by what he called an “illicit actor.”

ZachXBT published 11 Ethereum addresses tied to the theft.

Instant exchanges let users quickly swap one cryptocurrency for another, often with little identity verification, while Monero hides senders, receivers and amounts, making it a popular tool for obscuring stolen funds.

Swapping out of USDC and USDT also matters because the companies behind those stablecoins can freeze them.

ZachXBT said the culprit remains unidentified, but that he could say “with a high degree of confidence” that it isn’t Lazarus Group, citing multiple laundering indicators. Lazarus is the umbrella name often used for North Korea’s state-backed hackers. ZachXBT didn’t name the victim.

That sets the theft apart from the record-setting $6.4 billion Binance hack, which the FBI has attributed to TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking group.

In that case, stablecoin issuers moved within minutes. Tether froze stolen USDT on Ethereum in 37 minutes, and Circle froze all $823 million in stolen USDC, helping lock up about 44% of the haul, according to blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

North Korea’s launderers have since spread the remaining funds across about 12,000 wallets. It’s unclear whether any funds in the EU case have been frozen.

The incident adds to an already unprecedented week for crypto security. Beyond the record-breaking Binance hack, Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin just today reported halting withdrawals after a $40 million hack of its own. Likewise today, BTCPay Server told merchants to switch off their servers amid reports of hot wallet attacks.

#5039. AI poses greater threat to Bitcoin than quantum computing, Swan founder says

The Swan Bitcoin founder warned that hackers are using large language models to exploit security flaws with just a few prompts.

Artificial intelligence poses a more immediate threat to Bitcoin security than quantum computing, according to Swan Bitcoin founder Cory Klippsten, who warned that hackers are increasingly using AI to exploit vulnerabilities as crypto exchanges face a string of security breaches.

Speaking on Cointelegraph’s Chain Reaction, Klippsten dismissed much of the concern surrounding quantum computing as speculative, arguing that advances in large language models present a more pressing challenge as attackers use the technology to probe security weaknesses.

The warning comes as Brazil’s Mercado Bitcoin suspended deposits and withdrawals on Monday following a security incident, with researchers estimating that more than $46 million had left its wallets. Just days earlier, exchange Binance lost $6.3 billion in an Oct. 29 hack that the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) attributed to North Korean hackers.

Klippsten estimated that quantum computers capable of threatening Bitcoin’s cryptography remain at least a decade away, potentially longer. He argued that the network could adopt quantum-resistant protections if the threat became credible, but said rushing to implement them now would introduce unnecessary complexity.

He warned that cryptocurrency companies cannot rely on hastily developed software when safeguarding customer assets. “You need to actually know what the hell you’re doing from a security standpoint,” he said.

Klippsten also suggested that some cryptocurrency startups and investors had used fears surrounding quantum computing to attract funding, characterizing much of the discussion as fear, uncertainty and doubt (“FUD”) rather than an imminent security concern.

#5038. Bitcoin Firm Strive Climbs Up the Treasury Ranks With Another Big Buy

Bitcoin Firm Strive Climbs Up the Treasury Ranks With Another Big Buy

Strive, the Dallas-based company co-founded by Vivek Ramaswamy, now holds 43,124 Bitcoin after buying 124 BTC more last week. That stash is now worth roughly $3.3 billion, and good for about 0.2% of the 21 million coins that will ever exist.

That leaves it fourth among publicly traded companies, 390 coins behind Twenty One Capital (which holds 43,514 BTC) and 876 BTC behind Metaplanet (which holds 44,000 BTC), according to Bitcoin Treasuries data.

At Bitcoin’s price of about $77,000, those gaps are worth roughly $30 million and $67 million, respectively.

Strive is a Bitcoin treasury company—a publicly listed firm that keeps Bitcoin on its balance sheet, so anyone with a brokerage account can get exposure by buying its stock. Michael Saylor’s Strategy popularized the playbook back in 2020, and several other companies followed suit, including Strive.

Its coins cost an average of $87,785 each, or $3.8 billion in total, against a market value of $3.5 billion on Oct. 23, per its prospectus. Bitcoin has slipped since, so the pile is now worth a bit less than Strive paid for it.

The latest purchase covers Oct. 24 through Oct. 30 and was disclosed today in an SEC filing, the report US-listed companies must submit when something material happens. Strive paid about $10 million, an average of $80,526 per Bitcoin including fees, CEO Matt Cole said on X.

Strive acquired 124 $BTC for $10M at an average cost of $80,526 per bitcoin, bringing total holdings to ₿43,124.⁰⁰- Existing cash funded most purchases⁰- SATS ATM used for first time, representing 100% of weekly ATM proceeds⁰- Amplification Ratio: 62.7%⁰⁰$ASST $SATA $SATS pic.twitter.com/rFrS6y4AVc

— Matt Cole (@ColeMacro) November 2, 2026

Most of the money came from existing cash, which fell from $504.4 million to $490.4 million over the week. Strive also raised money for the first time by selling shares of a new security called SATS on the open market, Cole said, and that made up all of the week’s fundraising. It still holds 505,000 shares of Strategy’s preferred stock, known as STRC, worth about $50 million.

SATS is a preferred stock—a type of share that pays a fixed dividend before common shareholders see anything. Strive sold 7 million of them at $90 each in an October offering that raised about $630 million, and they carry a 15% annual dividend on a $100 stated value, paid every business day from Nov. 9.

On Oct. 26, Strive opened an at-the-market program—a way to sell new shares in small batches at the going price—for up to $1 billion more SATS. The prospectus says Strive expects to pay the dividends mainly by raising more capital, including selling more shares. The company has set aside 18 months of dividends in a reserve and reported no debt as of Oct. 23.

Strive’s amplification ratio, the preferred stock and debt it carries measured against the value of its Bitcoin, stood at 62.7%, or about $62.70 of obligations for every $100 of Bitcoin.

The company’s CEO has said ending 2026 as the second-largest public Bitcoin holder is possible. “I wouldn’t say that’s my base case,” Cole said on the One Chair podcast in September, “but I would say it’s in the possible set of cards.”

Saylor’s Strategy holds 849,327 Bitcoin, nearly 20 times Strive’s pile.

Twenty One Capital still holds the same 43,514 Bitcoin it had when co-founder Jack Mallers stepped down as CEO in July. His exit came as a plan by Tether to merge three Bitcoin businesses collapsed. The stock had fallen more than 80% from its highs, and his successor, Raphael Zagury, has said Twenty One should be judged on cash flow and capital discipline.

Metaplanet, a Tokyo-listed company that pivoted from hotels to Bitcoin in 2024, holds 44,000 Bitcoin after overtaking mining firm MARA in April. It has a stated goal of 100,000 by the end of 2026.

At last week’s pace of 124 Bitcoin, Strive would pass Twenty One in four weeks and Metaplanet in eight, assuming neither company buys more.

#5036. New Jersey drops Coinbase staking order ahead of crypto’s midterm test

New Jersey lifted its staking ban and dropped a $5 million penalty, clearing the way for Coinbase to restore staking access for users in the US state.

Update (Nov. 3 at 5:33 p.m. UTC): This article has been updated to include comments from Coinbase general counsel Molly Abraham.

New Jersey’s securities regulator has rescinded its 2023 cease-and-desist order against Coinbase’s staking service, lifting a ban that stopped residents staking new funds on the exchange for over three years.

The Oct. 30 notice, signed by Acting Bureau Chief Keith A. Alt, withdrew the order and its $5 million penalty without prejudice and without a ruling on the merits. The wording leaves the Bureau of Securities free to bring a fresh enforcement action against Coinbase in the future.

Coinbase disclosed the move in its third-quarter filing, stating the regulator informed the company on Oct. 29 of its plan to rescind. CEO Brian Armstrong said New Jersey users would regain staking access “very soon”.

California, Maryland, Wisconsin and Washington are still pursuing enforcement actions against the exchange with cases against the its staking services.

Staking involves committing cryptocurrency to help validate transactions and secure a blockchain in exchange for rewards. Companies like Coinbase handle the process for customers and take a share of the rewards as a fee.

Coinbase general counsel Molly Abraham said in a statement to Cointelegraph that New Jersey’s previous restrictions had prevented residents from earning an estimated $25 million in staking rewards. “Staking services are not securities. The SEC has made this clear,” Abraham said.

California led the 10-state task force behind the 2023 actions, which covered more than 600,000 accounts held by Californians.

Like New Jersey, California, Wisconsin and Maryland banned Coinbase from new staking while their cases ran. Washington’s regulator allows staking, but the ongoing case seeks to impose a $4 million fine on the exchange.

Coinbase estimates that California users have missed out on over $89 million in staking rewards since 2023, against more than $15 million in New Jersey.

The decision came ahead of the Nov. 3 US midterm elections, with prediction markets favoring Democrats to win the Senate control. A Democratic majority would put Sen. Elizabeth Warren, a prominent crypto critic, in line to chair the Senate Banking Committee, while Sen. Cynthia Lummis, a leading Republican advocate for the industry, is set to retire in January 2027.

New Jersey’s reversal came under Democratic Gov. Mikie Sherrill, who voted for both the CLARITY and GENIUS bills as a House member. The state’s Democratic senator, Cory Booker, also helped negotiate market structure legislation, though he opposed advancing CLARITY in September.

The industry has sought to build support across party lines ahead of the midterms. Fairshake and its affiliated super PACs are backing 19 Republicans and 14 Democrats in House races, while New Jersey’s Josh Gottheimer has also received crypto-industry support. The number of members of Congress with an A rating from advocacy group Stand With Crypto has risen to 332, up from 96 when the group launched in 2023, according to Reuters.
